Nin Hao: A Comprehensive Guide
January 26, 2025
Introduction: Understanding the Power of “Nín Hǎo” in ChineseIf you’re starting to learn Chinese, one of the first phrases you’ll come across is “Nín hǎo” (您好). This phrase is a respectful way of greeting someone, and it’s essential for building good relationships in both formal and informal settings. In this guide, we’ll explore what “Nín hǎo” means, how to pronounce it correctly, and how to write it in Chinese. Whether you’re a beginner or an intermediate learner, this article will help you understand the nuances of this important Chinese greeting.1. What Does “Nín Hǎo” Mean in Chinese?Nín hǎo (您好) is the polite and formal way to say “Hello” in Mandarin Chinese. It is often used to show respect to someone, especially when speaking to older people, authorities, or people you don’t know very well.1.1 Literal Meaning of “Nín Hǎo”Nín (您): This is the polite form of “you” in Chinese. It’s used to show respect and is equivalent to the formal “you” in English, like addressing someone as “Sir” or “Ma’am.”Hǎo (好): This means “good” or “well.” It’s the same character used in greetings like “nǐ hǎo (你好),” which simply means “Hello.”When combined, “Nín hǎo” literally means “You good” but carries a more formal and respectful tone compared to the casual “nǐ hǎo (你好)”.1.2 Usage in ContextNín hǎo is commonly used in situations where respect and politeness are important, such as when meeting older people, addressing a supervisor, or when you want to be more formal in professional settings.Example:Person A: 您好，李先生！ (Nín hǎo, Lǐ xiānsheng!) – “Hello, Mr. Li!”Person B: 您好！ (Nín hǎo!) – “Hello!”While “nǐ hǎo” is used in casual or familiar situations, “nín hǎo” adds a layer of respect, making it more appropriate for formal introductions or when you want to show politeness.2. How to Pronounce “Nín Hǎo” CorrectlyPronunciation is crucial in Chinese because of the language’s tonal nature. Mispronouncing tones can change the meaning of a word entirely. Here’s a breakdown of how to pronounce “nín hǎo” correctly:2.1 Pronouncing “Nín” (您)Nín (您) is pronounced with a rising tone. It sounds like “neen”, with the pitch rising slightly as you say it.The tone should be clear and distinct, and your voice should rise in pitch.2.2 Pronouncing “Hǎo” (好)Hǎo (好) is pronounced with a falling-rising tone, known as the third tone in Mandarin. It starts from a lower pitch and rises.The sound of “hǎo” is similar to saying “how” with a dip in your tone.2.3 Putting It Together“Nín hǎo” should be pronounced as “neen how”, but make sure to use the rising tone for “nín” and the falling-rising tone for “hǎo.”Practice tip: Try saying “nín hǎo” slowly at first, focusing on the correct tones. Gradually speed up as you get more comfortable with the pronunciation.3. How to Write “Nín Hǎo” in ChineseWriting “Nín hǎo” in Chinese requires knowledge of the individual characters and their proper strokes. Here’s a breakdown of each character:3.1 The Character “Nín” (您)Radicals: The character “nín” (您) is composed of two parts:亻: The “person” radical, meaning “person” or “human.”心: The “heart” radical, symbolizing emotions or thoughts.Stroke Order: The character has a relatively simple stroke order. Start with the left side, writing the “person” radical, then move to the heart component on the right.3.2 The Character “Hǎo” (好)Radicals: “Hǎo” (好) consists of two parts:女: The “woman” radical, symbolizing femininity or gentleness.子: The “child” radical, representing a person or offspring.Stroke Order: Start with the woman radical on the left, then the child radical on the right. Together, the character signifies “good” or “well.”4. How to Use “Nín Hǎo” in Everyday ConversationUnderstanding when and how to use “nín hǎo” is crucial for mastering polite conversation in Chinese. Here are some common scenarios where you might use this greeting:4.1 Formal IntroductionsIf you’re meeting someone for the first time, especially in a professional context, “nín hǎo” is the polite greeting you would use.Example:Person A: 您好，初次见面，我叫王明。 (Nín hǎo, chūcì jiànmiàn, wǒ jiào Wáng Míng.) – “Hello, nice to meet you. My name is Wang Ming.”4.2 Meeting Elders or SupervisorsWhen greeting someone older or in a higher position, “nín hǎo” is the appropriate phrase to show respect.Example:Person A: 您好，老师。 (Nín hǎo, lǎoshī.) – “Hello, teacher.”4.3 Customer Service or Business SettingsIn customer service or business contexts, using “nín hǎo” shows professionalism and respect toward clients or business partners.Example:Person A: 您好，欢迎光临。 (Nín hǎo, huānyíng guānglín.) – “Hello, welcome.”5. Key Differences Between “Nín Hǎo” and “Nǐ Hǎo”It’s important to understand the distinction between “nín hǎo” and “nǐ hǎo” (你好), as these two greetings are used in different contexts:5.1 “Nǐ Hǎo” (你好) – Casual and Informal“Nǐ hǎo” (你好) is the standard way to say “hello” in Mandarin and is used in casual settings.It’s appropriate for friends, family, or people you know well.5.2 “Nín Hǎo” (您好) – Polite and Formal“Nín hǎo” (您好), as explained, is the polite version. It’s used to show respect and is generally reserved for elders, strangers, or people in positions of authority.It’s always safe to use “nín hǎo” in formal situations, even if you’re unsure of the person’s age or status.6. Conclusion: Why “Nín Hǎo” is Essential for Chinese LearnersMastering “Nín hǎo” is an essential step in learning Mandarin, especially when it comes to showing respect and understanding the nuances of Chinese culture. Whether you’re greeting someone formally in a business meeting, meeting elders, or just want to make a good impression, “Nín hǎo” is a must-know phrase.By learning how to pronounce, write, and use “nín hǎo” appropriately, you’re well on your way to mastering the art of polite conversation in Chinese. So, go ahead and start using this phrase to impress your Chinese-speaking friends or colleagues with your cultural awareness!

Essentials
How to Say Hello in Chinese
By
Greg Rodgers
Greg Rodgers
Greg Rodgers is a freelance writer and photographer from Kentucky. He's been covering all things Asia for TripSavvy since 2010.
Travel's editorial guidelines
Updated on June 23, 2024
In This Article
View All
In This Article
The Difference Between Chinese and Mandarin
The Easiest Way to Say Hello
Saying Hello in Formal Occasions
Simple Responses in Chinese
How to Say Hello in Cantonese
Should I Bow When Saying Hello in Chinese?
How to Say Cheers in Chinese
Photo: Maremagnum / Getty Images
Knowing how to say hello in Mandarin and Cantonese allows you to properly greet more than 1.4 billion people who speak the languages. These basic greetings will help in your travels through China, Hong Kong, Macau, and Taiwan and will be understood in Mandarin- and Cantonese-speaking communities worldwide. Mandarin is the most widely spoken language in the world, so knowing how to say "hello" is extremely useful!
Mandarin and Cantonese are notoriously difficult languages to master, and native speakers of those languages know this and appreciate the effort. A relatively short word can take on completely different meanings depending on which of the four tones in Mandarin is used (or six tones in Cantonese!). Pinyin, a Romanization system for the Chinese characters, makes it easier to "read" Chinese, but you'll need to leave your English pronunciation habits behind.
Fortunately, tones aren't much of an issue for learning simple ways to say hello in Chinese. Context helps. You'll always be understood and will get plenty of smiles for the effort, particularly if you utilize a few tips for communicating with Chinese speakers.
TripSavvy / Ellie Yang
The Difference Between Chinese and Mandarin
Mandarin is a form of Chinese spoken in Mainland China. Although there are eight dialects and more than 200 languages spoken in China, Mandarin is the common language, spoken by nearly everyone. Cantonese is spoken in the provinces of Guangdong and Guangxi and in Hong Kong and Macau. You will encounter Mandarin while traveling in Beijing, and because it is the "speech of officials," knowing how to say hello in Mandarin is useful everywhere you go.
Mandarin has four tones:
First tone: flat (mā means "mother")Second tone: rising (má means "hemp")Third tone: falling then rising (mǎ means "horse")Fourth tone: falling (mà means "scold")No tone: Ma with a neutral/no tone turns a statement into a question.
Tones can entirely change the meaning of a word. As the above example with (ma) shows, using the wrong tones at the wrong times can cause serious confusion.
As for reading and writing, don't feel bad if you're baffled when confronted with Chinese characters—they're difficult! That's why we begin by learning how to use Pinyin.
The Easiest Way to Say Hello in Chinese
Ni hao (pronounced "nee haow") is the basic, default greeting in Mandarin Chinese. It is written as 你好 (nǐ hǎo). Ni hao's meaning is, literally, "you OK/good."
Although both words in Pinyin are marked as third tone (nǐ hǎo), the pronunciation changes a bit because two third tones occur back to back. In this instance, the first word (nǐ) is pronounced with a second tone that rises in pitch while the second word (hǎo) keeps the third tone and is pronounced with a "dip," a falling-then-rising tone.
Some people, particularly in Taiwan, choose to enhance the greeting by adding the interrogative "ma" to the end to form "ni hao ma?" Turning "you good" into a friendly "how are you?" But this isn't used as often in Beijing as language guides seem to think it is. When traveling in mainland China, a simple ni hao will suffice!
You will probably hear "hi" and "hello" often when being greeted as a Westerner in Beijing. You can reply with ni hao for practice and to be polite.
Saying Hello in Formal Occasions
Following the concept of saving face in Asia, elders and those of higher social status should always be shown a little extra respect. Adding just one additional letter (ni becomes nin) will make your greeting a bit more formal. Use nín hăo (pronounced "neen haow")—a more polite variation of the standard greeting—when greeting older people. The first word used is still a rising tone.
You can also make nin hao into "how are you?" by adding the question word ma to the end for nin hao ma?
Simple Responses in Chinese
The easiest reply to ni hao is offering a ni hao in return, but taking the greeting one step further is sure to get a smile during the interaction. Regardless, you should reply with something—not acknowledging someone's friendly ni hao is bad etiquette.
Hǎo: goodHěn hǎo: very goodBù hǎo: not good (bad)Xiè xie: thank you (pronounced similar to "zh-yeh zh-yeh" with two falling tones) is optional and can be added to the end.Nǐ ne: and you? (pronounced "nee nuh")
A simple greeting sequence could proceed like this:
You: Ni hao! (hello)
Friend: Ni hao ma? (how are you?)
You: Wo hen hao! Xie xie. Ni ne? (I am very good, thanks. And you?)
Friend: Hao. Xie xie. (Good. Thanks.)
How to Say Hello in Cantonese
Although it seems similar to Mandarin, Cantonese is actually quite different with six different tones instead of four. However, when it comes to greetings, saying hello in Cantonese is quite similar to Mandarin. Neih hou (pronounced "nay hoe") replaces ni hao. Both words have a rising tone.
Note: Although neih hou ma? is grammatically correct, it is uncommon to say this in Cantonese.
A common response in Cantonese is gei hou which means "fine."
Given Hong Kong's English history, you'll often hear "ha-lo" as a friendly hello! But reserve "ha-lo" for casual and informal situations. All other times, you should be saying neih hou.
Should I Bow When Saying Hello in Chinese?
No, absolutely not. Unlike in Japan where bowing is common, people tend to only bow in China during martial arts, as an apology, or to show deep respect at funerals. Many Chinese people opt to shake hands, but don't expect the usual firm, Western-style handshake. Eye contact and a smile are important.
Although bowing in China is rare, make sure you return one if you receive a bow. As with bowing in Japan, maintaining eye contact as you bow is seen as a martial arts challenge!
How to Say Cheers in Chinese
After saying hello in Chinese, you may end up making new friends—particularly if you're at a banquet or in a bar. Be prepared; there are some rules for proper drinking etiquette. You should certainly know how to say cheers in Chinese! The default toast in China is ganbei (sounds like: “gon bay”) which literally means "dry cup."
Along with knowing how to say hello, learning some useful phrases in Mandarin before traveling in China is a good idea.

Nin Hao: A Comprehensive Guide

Introduction: Understanding the Power of “Nín Hǎo” in ChineseIf you’re starting to learn Chinese, one of the first phrases you’ll come across is “Nín hǎo” (您好). What Does “Nín Hǎo” Mean in Chinese?Nín hǎo (您好) is the polite and formal way to say “Hello” in Mandarin Chinese. It is often used to show respect to someone, especially when speaking to older people, authorities, or people you don’t know very well.1.1 Literal Meaning of “Nín Hǎo”Nín (您): This is the polite form of “you” in Chinese. It’s used to show respect and is equivalent to the formal “you” in English, like addressing someone as “Sir” or “Ma’am.”Hǎo (好): This means “good” or “well.” It’s the same character used in greetings like “nǐ hǎo (你好),” which simply means “Hello.”When combined, “Nín hǎo” literally means “You good” but carries a more formal and respectful tone compared to the casual “nǐ hǎo (你好)”.1.2 Usage in ContextNín hǎo is commonly used in situations where respect and politeness are important, such as when meeting older people, addressing a supervisor, or when you want to be more formal in professional settings.Example:Person A: 您好，李先生！ (Nín hǎo, Lǐ xiānsheng!) – “Hello, Mr. Li!”Person B: 您好！ (Nín hǎo!) – “Hello!”While “nǐ hǎo” is used in casual or familiar situations, “nín hǎo” adds a layer of respect, making it more appropriate for formal introductions or when you want to show politeness.2. How to Pronounce “Nín Hǎo” CorrectlyPronunciation is crucial in Chinese because of the language’s tonal nature. Mispronouncing tones can change the meaning of a word entirely. Here’s a breakdown of how to pronounce “nín hǎo” correctly:2.1 Pronouncing “Nín” (您)Nín (您) is pronounced with a rising tone. It sounds like “neen”, with the pitch rising slightly as you say it.The tone should be clear and distinct, and your voice should rise in pitch.2.2 Pronouncing “Hǎo” (好)Hǎo (好) is pronounced with a falling-rising tone, known as the third tone in Mandarin. It starts from a lower pitch and rises.The sound of “hǎo” is similar to saying “how” with a dip in your tone.2.3 Putting It Together“Nín hǎo” should be pronounced as “neen how”, but make sure to use the rising tone for “nín” and the falling-rising tone for “hǎo.”Practice tip: Try saying “nín hǎo” slowly at first, focusing on the correct tones. Gradually speed up as you get more comfortable with the pronunciation.3. How to Write “Nín Hǎo” in ChineseWriting “Nín hǎo” in Chinese requires knowledge of the individual characters and their proper strokes. Here’s a breakdown of each character:3.1 The Character “Nín” (您)Radicals: The character “nín” (您) is composed of two parts:亻: The “person” radical, meaning “person” or “human.”心: The “heart” radical, symbolizing emotions or thoughts.Stroke Order: The character has a relatively simple stroke order. Start with the left side, writing the “person” radical, then move to the heart component on the right.3.2 The Character “Hǎo” (好)Radicals: “Hǎo” (好) consists of two parts:女: The “woman” radical, symbolizing femininity or gentleness.子: The “child” radical, representing a person or offspring.Stroke Order: Start with the woman radical on the left, then the child radical on the right. Together, the character signifies “good” or “well.”4. How to Use “Nín Hǎo” in Everyday ConversationUnderstanding when and how to use “nín hǎo” is crucial for mastering polite conversation in Chinese. Here are some common scenarios where you might use this greeting:4.1 Formal IntroductionsIf you’re meeting someone for the first time, especially in a professional context, “nín hǎo” is the polite greeting you would use.Example:Person A: 您好，初次见面，我叫王明。 (Nín hǎo, chūcì jiànmiàn, wǒ jiào Wáng Míng.) – “Hello, nice to meet you. My name is Wang Ming.”4.2 Meeting Elders or SupervisorsWhen greeting someone older or in a higher position, “nín hǎo” is the appropriate phrase to show respect.Example:Person A: 您好，老师。 (Nín hǎo, lǎoshī.) – “Hello, teacher.”4.3 Customer Service or Business SettingsIn customer service or business contexts, using “nín hǎo” shows professionalism and respect toward clients or business partners.Example:Person A: 您好，欢迎光临。 (Nín hǎo, huānyíng guānglín.) – “Hello, welcome.”5. Key Differences Between “Nín Hǎo” and “Nǐ Hǎo”It’s important to understand the distinction between “nín hǎo” and “nǐ hǎo” (你好), as these two greetings are used in different contexts:5.1 “Nǐ Hǎo” (你好) – Casual and Informal“Nǐ hǎo” (你好) is the standard way to say “hello” in Mandarin and is used in casual settings.It’s appropriate for friends, family, or people you know well.5.2 “Nín Hǎo” (您好) – Polite and Formal“Nín hǎo” (您好), as explained, is the polite version. It’s used to show respect and is generally reserved for elders, strangers, or people in positions of authority.It’s always safe to use “nín hǎo” in formal situations, even if you’re unsure of the person’s age or status.6. Conclusion: Why “Nín Hǎo” is Essential for Chinese LearnersMastering “Nín hǎo” is an essential step in learning Mandarin, especially when it comes to showing respect and understanding the nuances of Chinese culture. Whether you’re greeting someone formally in a business meeting, meeting elders, or just want to make a good impression, “Nín hǎo” is a must-know phrase.By learning how to pronounce, write, and use “nín hǎo” appropriately, you’re well on your way to mastering the art of polite conversation in Chinese. So, go ahead and start using this phrase to impress your Chinese-speaking friends or colleagues with your cultural awareness!

Nin Hao: A Comprehensive Guide
January 26, 2025
Introduction: Understanding the Power of “Nín Hǎo” in ChineseIf you’re starting to learn Chinese, one of the first phrases you’ll come across is “Nín hǎo” (您好). This phrase is a respectful way of greeting someone, and it’s essential for building good relationships in both formal and informal settings. In this guide, we’ll explore what “Nín hǎo” means, how to pronounce it correctly, and how to write it in Chinese. Whether you’re a beginner or an intermediate learner, this article will help you understand the nuances of this important Chinese greeting.1. What Does “Nín Hǎo” Mean in Chinese?Nín hǎo (您好) is the polite and formal way to say “Hello” in Mandarin Chinese. It is often used to show respect to someone, especially when speaking to older people, authorities, or people you don’t know very well.1.1 Literal Meaning of “Nín Hǎo”Nín (您): This is the polite form of “you” in Chinese. It’s used to show respect and is equivalent to the formal “you” in English, like addressing someone as “Sir” or “Ma’am.”Hǎo (好): This means “good” or “well.” It’s the same character used in greetings like “nǐ hǎo (你好),” which simply means “Hello.”When combined, “Nín hǎo” literally means “You good” but carries a more formal and respectful tone compared to the casual “nǐ hǎo (你好)”.1.2 Usage in ContextNín hǎo is commonly used in situations where respect and politeness are important, such as when meeting older people, addressing a supervisor, or when you want to be more formal in professional settings.Example:Person A: 您好，李先生！ (Nín hǎo, Lǐ xiānsheng!) – “Hello, Mr. Li!”Person B: 您好！ (Nín hǎo!) – “Hello!”While “nǐ hǎo” is used in casual or familiar situations, “nín hǎo” adds a layer of respect, making it more appropriate for formal introductions or when you want to show politeness.2. How to Pronounce “Nín Hǎo” CorrectlyPronunciation is crucial in Chinese because of the language’s tonal nature. Mispronouncing tones can change the meaning of a word entirely. Here’s a breakdown of how to pronounce “nín hǎo” correctly:2.1 Pronouncing “Nín” (您)Nín (您) is pronounced with a rising tone. It sounds like “neen”, with the pitch rising slightly as you say it.The tone should be clear and distinct, and your voice should rise in pitch.2.2 Pronouncing “Hǎo” (好)Hǎo (好) is pronounced with a falling-rising tone, known as the third tone in Mandarin. It starts from a lower pitch and rises.The sound of “hǎo” is similar to saying “how” with a dip in your tone.2.3 Putting It Together“Nín hǎo” should be pronounced as “neen how”, but make sure to use the rising tone for “nín” and the falling-rising tone for “hǎo.”Practice tip: Try saying “nín hǎo” slowly at first, focusing on the correct tones. Gradually speed up as you get more comfortable with the pronunciation.3. How to Write “Nín Hǎo” in ChineseWriting “Nín hǎo” in Chinese requires knowledge of the individual characters and their proper strokes. Here’s a breakdown of each character:3.1 The Character “Nín” (您)Radicals: The character “nín” (您) is composed of two parts:亻: The “person” radical, meaning “person” or “human.”心: The “heart” radical, symbolizing emotions or thoughts.Stroke Order: The character has a relatively simple stroke order. Start with the left side, writing the “person” radical, then move to the heart component on the right.3.2 The Character “Hǎo” (好)Radicals: “Hǎo” (好) consists of two parts:女: The “woman” radical, symbolizing femininity or gentleness.子: The “child” radical, representing a person or offspring.Stroke Order: Start with the woman radical on the left, then the child radical on the right. Together, the character signifies “good” or “well.”4. How to Use “Nín Hǎo” in Everyday ConversationUnderstanding when and how to use “nín hǎo” is crucial for mastering polite conversation in Chinese. Here are some common scenarios where you might use this greeting:4.1 Formal IntroductionsIf you’re meeting someone for the first time, especially in a professional context, “nín hǎo” is the polite greeting you would use.Example:Person A: 您好，初次见面，我叫王明。 (Nín hǎo, chūcì jiànmiàn, wǒ jiào Wáng Míng.) – “Hello, nice to meet you. My name is Wang Ming.”4.2 Meeting Elders or SupervisorsWhen greeting someone older or in a higher position, “nín hǎo” is the appropriate phrase to show respect.Example:Person A: 您好，老师。 (Nín hǎo, lǎoshī.) – “Hello, teacher.”4.3 Customer Service or Business SettingsIn customer service or business contexts, using “nín hǎo” shows professionalism and respect toward clients or business partners.Example:Person A: 您好，欢迎光临。 (Nín hǎo, huānyíng guānglín.) – “Hello, welcome.”5. Key Differences Between “Nín Hǎo” and “Nǐ Hǎo”It’s important to understand the distinction between “nín hǎo” and “nǐ hǎo” (你好), as these two greetings are used in different contexts:5.1 “Nǐ Hǎo” (你好) – Casual and Informal“Nǐ hǎo” (你好) is the standard way to say “hello” in Mandarin and is used in casual settings.It’s appropriate for friends, family, or people you know well.5.2 “Nín Hǎo” (您好) – Polite and Formal“Nín hǎo” (您好), as explained, is the polite version. It’s used to show respect and is generally reserved for elders, strangers, or people in positions of authority.It’s always safe to use “nín hǎo” in formal situations, even if you’re unsure of the person’s age or status.6. Conclusion: Why “Nín Hǎo” is Essential for Chinese LearnersMastering “Nín hǎo” is an essential step in learning Mandarin, especially when it comes to showing respect and understanding the nuances of Chinese culture. Whether you’re greeting someone formally in a business meeting, meeting elders, or just want to make a good impression, “Nín hǎo” is a must-know phrase.By learning how to pronounce, write, and use “nín hǎo” appropriately, you’re well on your way to mastering the art of polite conversation in Chinese. So, go ahead and start using this phrase to impress your Chinese-speaking friends or colleagues with your cultural awareness!

How to Say Hello in Chinese
By
Greg Rodgers
Greg Rodgers
Greg Rodgers is a freelance writer and photographer from Kentucky. He's been covering all things Asia for TripSavvy since 2010.

How to Say Hello in Cantonese
Should I Bow When Saying Hello in Chinese?
How to Say Cheers in Chinese
Photo: Maremagnum / Getty Images
Knowing how to say hello in Mandarin and Cantonese allows you to properly greet more than 1.4 billion people who speak the languages. These basic greetings will help in your travels through China, Hong Kong, Macau, and Taiwan and will be understood in Mandarin- and Cantonese-speaking communities worldwide. Mandarin is the most widely spoken language in the world, so knowing how to say "hello" is extremely useful!
Mandarin and Cantonese are notoriously difficult languages to master, and native speakers of those languages know this and appreciate the effort. A relatively short word can take on completely different meanings depending on which of the four tones in Mandarin is used (or six tones in Cantonese!). Pinyin, a Romanization system for the Chinese characters, makes it easier to "read" Chinese, but you'll need to leave your English pronunciation habits behind.
Fortunately, tones aren't much of an issue for learning simple ways to say hello in Chinese. Context helps. You'll always be understood and will get plenty of smiles for the effort, particularly if you utilize a few tips for communicating with Chinese speakers.
TripSavvy / Ellie Yang
The Difference Between Chinese and Mandarin
Mandarin is a form of Chinese spoken in Mainland China. Although there are eight dialects and more than 200 languages spoken in China, Mandarin is the common language, spoken by nearly everyone. Cantonese is spoken in the provinces of Guangdong and Guangxi and in Hong Kong and Macau. You will encounter Mandarin while traveling in Beijing, and because it is the "speech of officials," knowing how to say hello in Mandarin is useful everywhere you go.
Mandarin has four tones:
First tone: flat (mā means "mother")Second tone: rising (má means "hemp")Third tone: falling then rising (mǎ means "horse")Fourth tone: falling (mà means "scold")No tone: Ma with a neutral/no tone turns a statement into a question.
Tones can entirely change the meaning of a word. As the above example with (ma) shows, using the wrong tones at the wrong times can cause serious confusion.
As for reading and writing, don't feel bad if you're baffled when confronted with Chinese characters—they're difficult! That's why we begin by learning how to use Pinyin.
The Easiest Way to Say Hello in Chinese
Ni hao (pronounced "nee haow") is the basic, default greeting in Mandarin Chinese. It is written as 你好 (nǐ hǎo). Ni hao's meaning is, literally, "you OK/good."
Although both words in Pinyin are marked as third tone (nǐ hǎo), the pronunciation changes a bit because two third tones occur back to back. In this instance, the first word (nǐ) is pronounced with a second tone that rises in pitch while the second word (hǎo) keeps the third tone and is pronounced with a "dip," a falling-then-rising tone.
Some people, particularly in Taiwan, choose to enhance the greeting by adding the interrogative "ma" to the end to form "ni hao ma?" Turning "you good" into a friendly "how are you?" But this isn't used as often in Beijing as language guides seem to think it is. When traveling in mainland China, a simple ni hao will suffice!
You will probably hear "hi" and "hello" often when being greeted as a Westerner in Beijing. You can reply with ni hao for practice and to be polite.
Saying Hello in Formal Occasions
Following the concept of saving face in Asia, elders and those of higher social status should always be shown a little extra respect. Adding just one additional letter (ni becomes nin) will make your greeting a bit more formal. Use nín hăo (pronounced "neen haow")—a more polite variation of the standard greeting—when greeting older people. The first word used is still a rising tone.
You can also make nin hao into "how are you?" by adding the question word ma to the end for nin hao ma?
Simple Responses in Chinese
The easiest reply to ni hao is offering a ni hao in return, but taking the greeting one step further is sure to get a smile during the interaction. Regardless, you should reply with something—not acknowledging someone's friendly ni hao is bad etiquette.
Hǎo: goodHěn hǎo: very goodBù hǎo: not good (bad)Xiè xie: thank you (pronounced similar to "zh-yeh zh-yeh" with two falling tones) is optional and can be added to the end.Nǐ ne: and you? (pronounced "nee nuh")
A simple greeting sequence could proceed like this:
You: Ni hao! (hello)
Friend: Ni hao ma? (how are you?)
You: Wo hen hao! Xie xie. Ni ne? (I am very good, thanks. And you?)
Friend: Hao. Xie xie. (Good. Thanks.)
How to Say Hello in Cantonese
Although it seems similar to Mandarin, Cantonese is actually quite different with six different tones instead of four. However, when it comes to greetings, saying hello in Cantonese is quite similar to Mandarin. Neih hou (pronounced "nay hoe") replaces ni hao. Both words have a rising tone.
Note: Although neih hou ma? is grammatically correct, it is uncommon to say this in Cantonese.
A common response in Cantonese is gei hou which means "fine."
Given Hong Kong's English history, you'll often hear "ha-lo" as a friendly hello! But reserve "ha-lo" for casual and informal situations. All other times, you should be saying neih hou.
Should I Bow When Saying Hello in Chinese?
No, absolutely not. Unlike in Japan where bowing is common, people tend to only bow in China during martial arts, as an apology, or to show deep respect at funerals. Many Chinese people opt to shake hands, but don't expect the usual firm, Western-style handshake. Eye contact and a smile are important.
Although bowing in China is rare, make sure you return one if you receive a bow. As with bowing in Japan, maintaining eye contact as you bow is seen as a martial arts challenge!
How to Say Cheers in Chinese
After saying hello in Chinese, you may end up making new friends—particularly if you're at a banquet or in a bar. Be prepared; there are some rules for proper drinking etiquette. You should certainly know how to say cheers in Chinese! The default toast in China is ganbei (sounds like: “gon bay”) which literally means "dry cup."
Along with knowing how to say hello, learning some useful phrases in Mandarin before traveling in China is a good idea.

标题: Ni Hao Ma Meaning: What Does This Chinese Greeting Mean?
URL: https://spiritmeans.com/ni-hao-ma-meaning/

Ni Hao Ma Meaning: What Does This Chinese Greeting Mean? - Spirit Means

Ni Hao Ma Meaning: What Does This Chinese Greeting Mean?
Published by
Molly Famwat
on
February 9, 2025
When learning Mandarin Chinese, one of the first phrases you encounter is “Nǐ hǎo ma?” This phrase is widely used in both casual and formal settings. Understanding the Ni Hao Ma meaning is essential for anyone interested in Chinese culture, travel, or language.
Breaking Down Ni Hao Ma
The phrase “Nǐ hǎo ma?” consists of three characters. Each has a specific role in forming this polite question.
Nǐ (\u4f60) means “you.”
Hǎo (\u597d) translates to “good.”
Ma (\u5417) is a question particle that turns a statement into a yes/no question.
So, the literal translation of “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is “Are you good?” In English, it corresponds to “How are you?” This basic greeting is a gateway to more complex Chinese conversations. Knowing the Ni Hao Ma meaning can help you connect with Mandarin speakers worldwide.
Also Read: Dreaming About Teeth Falling out
How to Use Ni Hao Ma in Conversation
In daily conversations, “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is used similarly to “How are you?” in English. It’s a polite and friendly way to check in on someone’s well-being. Here are some common responses to this question:
Wǒ hěn hǎo (\u6211\u5f88\u597d) – “I am good.”
Mǒ hǎo (\u4e0d\u597d) – “Not good.”
Hái hǎo (\u8fd8\u597d) – “So-so” or “Okay.”
Once you understand the Ni Hao Ma meaning, you can easily use it in various situations. Whether greeting a friend or meeting someone new, this phrase is a perfect conversation starter.
Cultural Context of Ni Hao Ma
While “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is a standard greeting, it’s not always the go-to in all Chinese-speaking regions. In some areas, people might use different phrases based on familiarity or local dialects. For example:
Chī le ma? (\u5403\u4e86\u5417?) – “Have you eaten?”
Zǐn me yàng? (\u600e\u4e48\u6837?) – “How’s it going?”
Despite these variations, the Ni Hao Ma meaning remains a fundamental part of basic Mandarin. It reflects not just language but also the culture’s emphasis on politeness and mutual respect.
When to Use Ni Hao Ma
Understanding when to use “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is just as important as knowing its meaning. This phrase is versatile and fits into many scenarios, such as:
Meeting someone for the first time.
Greeting colleagues or classmates.
Starting a casual chat with friends.
However, in very formal situations, people might opt for more formal greetings like “Nǐn hǎo” (\u60a8\u597d), where “Nǐn” is the formal version of “you.”
Comparing Ni Hao Ma with Other Greetings
To better understand the Ni Hao Ma meaning, let’s compare it with other greetings in Mandarin. Here is a simple table showing different greetings and their contexts:
Chinese PhrasePinyinEnglish TranslationWhen to Use你好吗?Nǐ hǎo ma?How are you?General, casual settings你好Nǐ hǎoHelloUniversal, formal or informal吃了吗?Chī le ma?Have you eaten?Informal, friendly settings怎么样?Zǐn me yàng?How’s it going?Casual, familiar relationships您好Nǐn hǎoHello (formal)Formal situations
From this table, it’s clear that while “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is common, there are many ways to greet someone in Mandarin. Knowing the Ni Hao Ma meaning helps you navigate these options.
Role of Tone in Ni Hao Ma
Mandarin is a tonal language, which means the pitch or intonation used can change a word’s meaning. The phrase “Nǐ hǎo ma?” involves specific tones that are important for clarity.
Nǐ (\u4f60) has a third tone, which falls and then rises.
Hǎo (\u597d) also has a third tone.
Ma (\u5417) is a neutral tone, pronounced lightly at the end.
Getting the tones right ensures that your greeting is understood. Mispronouncing tones can lead to confusion, as Mandarin has many words that sound similar but mean different things. Mastering the tones adds depth to your understanding of the Ni Hao Ma meaning and improves your overall fluency.
Ni Hao Ma in Media and Pop Culture
The phrase “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is so iconic that it often appears in Chinese media, TV shows, and even Western films portraying Chinese culture. Characters might use it to emphasize a friendly or polite tone. It’s also common in textbooks, language apps, and courses designed to teach Mandarin. Understanding the Ni Hao Ma meaning helps you appreciate these cultural references.
Learning Ni Hao Ma as a Beginner
If you’re new to learning Chinese, starting with basic phrases like “Nǐ hǎo ma?” builds a strong foundation. Repetition and practice are key. Try using it in daily greetings with language partners or during travel.
Many language apps and online resources use “Nǐ hǎo ma?” in their introductory lessons. Understanding the Ni Hao Ma meaning early on boosts your confidence and makes learning more engaging.
Also Read: XAI770K Meaning
Common Mistakes When Using Ni Hao Ma
While “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is straightforward, beginners often make a few mistakes. These include mispronouncing tones, using it in overly formal contexts, or overusing it when other greetings might fit better. Being aware of these pitfalls enhances your communication skills.
For example, constantly saying “Nǐ hǎo ma?” in formal business settings might seem too casual. In such cases, switching to “Nǐn hǎo” shows respect.
Why Knowing Ni Hao Ma Meaning Matters
Understanding the Ni Hao Ma meaning is more than just learning a phrase. It opens doors to deeper cultural connections and smoother interactions. Whether you’re traveling, working with Chinese-speaking colleagues, or studying the language, this phrase is a valuable tool.
It’s also a sign of respect when you greet someone in their native language. Even if your Mandarin skills are basic, using “Nǐ hǎo ma?” correctly shows that you’re making an effort to connect.
Expanding Beyond Ni Hao Ma
Once you’re comfortable with “Nǐ hǎo ma?”, you can expand your vocabulary to include other greetings and expressions. This helps you sound more natural and versatile in conversation. For example:
Nǐ jǐn tiān zěn me yàng? (\u4f60\u4eca\u5929\u600e\u4e48\u6837?) – “How’s your day?”
Zuì jìn hǎo ma? (\u6700\u8fd1\u597d\u5417?) – “How have you been recently?”
Each new phrase builds on the foundation set by understanding the Ni Hao Ma meaning. This gradual approach makes language learning more manageable and enjoyable.
Frequently Asked Questions
What does “Ni Hao Ma” mean in English?
“Ni Hao Ma” (你好吗?) translates to “How are you?” in English. It’s a common Mandarin greeting used to ask about someone’s well-being.
How do you respond to “Ni Hao Ma”?
You can reply with “Wǒ hěn hǎo” (我很好) meaning “I am good” or “Mǎmǎ hūhū” (马马虎虎) meaning “So-so.”
Is “Ni Hao Ma” used in formal situations?
“Ni Hao Ma” is generally used in casual settings. For formal situations, people often say “Nín hǎo” (您好) which is a more respectful greeting.
What is the correct pronunciation of “Ni Hao Ma”?
It is pronounced as [Nee How Ma], with rising and falling tones on “Ni” and “Hao.” Correct tones are important in Mandarin for clear communication.
Conclusion
In summary, the phrase “Nǐ hǎo ma?” is a simple yet powerful tool in Mandarin Chinese. Knowing the Ni Hao Ma meaning not only helps you greet others politely but also deepens your understanding of Chinese language and culture. From daily conversations to cultural exchanges, this phrase plays an important role in connecting people. By mastering its pronunciation, context, and variations, you can confidently engage in meaningful interactions with Mandarin speakers around the world.

How to Say Hello in Chinese Mandarin: A Guide to 您好 vs 你好
# 您好 vs 你好
# Chinese learning
Demi
5.4k
0
09/11/2024
Share
In learning Mandarin Chinese, mastering greetings is a fundamental step in developing Chinese skills and cultural understanding. Among the most common ways to say “hello” are “您好” (nín hǎo) and “你好” (nǐ hǎo). While both expressions serve the same purpose, they differ significantly in terms of formality and context.
Knowing when to use each greeting is crucial, as it reflects your understanding of social norms and respect within Chinese culture.
Common Ways to Say Hi in Chinese
Greetings are an essential part of communication in any language. Knowing how to say “hi” in different contexts can help you make a positive impression and foster connections with others.
By familiarizing yourself with these expressions, you’ll be better equipped to navigate social interactions in Mandarin.
“你好” (nǐ hǎo)
Meaning: “你好” translates to “hello” in English. Composed of the characters for “you” (你 nĭ) and “good” (好 hǎo), it literally means “you good.” It is pronounced as Ni Hao. It is a casual greeting used among friends, family, and acquaintances.
Usage: This phrase is appropriate for informal situations. It is commonly used in daily conversations, social gatherings, and casual interactions. For example, you would say “你好” when meeting a friend or a peer.
“您好” (nín hǎo)
Meaning: “您好” is a more formal or polite form to say “hi.” The character “您” (nín) is the polite form of “you,” used to show respect. However, “Ni hao” is slightly more familiar (and the more common one) but is not necessarily impolite.
Usage: This greeting is suitable for formal situations, for example, addressing elders, superiors, or people you do not know well. You might use it in a business meeting, when speaking to a teacher, or when interacting with customers in a service setting.
“大家好” (dàjiā hǎo)
Meaning: “大家好” translates to “Hello everyone” and is used to greet a group of people. The phrase combines “大” (dà), meaning “big” or “great,” “家” (jiā), meaning “family” or “everyone,” and “好” (hǎo), meaning “good” or “well.”
Usage: This greeting is suitable for informal situations where you are addressing a group, such as friends, classmates, or colleagues. You might use it in social gatherings, meetings, or public speeches to create a friendly and inclusive atmosphere.
Different Scenarios: 您好 & 你好
Greetings are essential for establishing connections and showing respect. Grasping the differences in Chinese greetings can greatly enhance your relationships with others and showcase your appreciation for Chinese culture.
Example 1: Meeting a Friend
Greeting: “你好, 你最近怎么样?” Translation: “Hello, how have you been lately?”
You can say “你好” in this casual scenario. “你好” is perfect for greeting a friend. It establishes a friendly tone and encourages open conversation.
Example 2: Greeting a Teacher
Greeting: “您好, 老师.” Translation: “Hello, teacher.”
In this case, “您好” is appropriate as it shows respect for the teacher’s authority and experience. It acknowledges the teacher-student relationship.
Example 3: Customer Service
Greeting: “您好, 请问有什么我可以帮助您的吗?” Translation: “Hello, how can I assist you?”
If you were addressing someone in a formal setting, perhaps you’re going to an interview, you would want to treat the interviewer with respect, so you can say “您好” to demonstrate professionalism and respect for the customer.
Learn authentic Chinese from those who live and breathe the culture.
Specially tailored for kids aged 3-18 around the world!
Get started free!
How to Say Hi at Different Times of Day
Greetings can vary depending on the time of day, and using the appropriate phrase is important in Chinese. Here are some common ways to greet at different times:
早上好 (zǎoshang hǎo)
Meaning: “Good morning.”
Usage: Used in the morning, typically until around noon. It’s a friendly way to greet people as the day begins. It’s also possible to say 早 (zǎo), which also means good morning.
下午好 (xiàwǔ hǎo)
Meaning: “Good afternoon.”
Usage: Used from around noon until the early evening. It’s a polite form to acknowledge someone during the afternoon hours.
晚上好 (wǎnshang hǎo)
Meaning: “Good evening.”
Usage: Used in the evening, generally after sunset. It’s appropriate for social gatherings or formal occasions during the night.
How to Say Hello on the phone
When answering the phone in Mandarin Chinese, it’s important to use appropriate greetings that convey politeness and clarity.
Common Ways to Answer the Phone in Chinese
喂 (wèi): This is the most common way to answer the phone in Chinese. It is an informal greeting that is widely recognized and used across various contexts.
The simplicity and versatility of “喂” make it suitable for most situations, whether you are speaking with friends, family, or acquaintances.
When a person pick up the phone and say “喂,” it sets a casual tone for the conversation, signaling to the caller that you are ready to engage. It’s particularly effective in everyday interactions, as it fosters a sense of familiarity and comfort.
Example Conversations
When a person answers the phone with the right greeting, it sets a positive tone for the conversation and reflects their understanding of social etiquette in Mandarin.
Answering a Call from a Friend:
You: “喂! 你好吗?” (Wèi! Nǐ hǎo ma?)
Translation: “Hello! How are you?”
Answering a Business Call:
You: “您好, 请问有什么可以帮助您的?” (Nín hǎo, qǐngwèn yǒu shénme kěyǐ bāngzhù nín de?)
Translation: “Hello, how can I assist you?”
Summary
This guide introduces how to greet in Chinese properly, exploring the difference between the two common phrases “你好” and “您好”.
By understanding the appropriate contexts for each greeting, learners of Mandarin can effectively navigate social hierarchies and convey their respect to others through the language.
If you have a passion for Chinese unique culture or want to learn Chinese, Wukong Chinese Courses will be your top choice. We offer high-quality online Chinese teacher and a variety of engaging activities that make language learning easier and more enjoyable.
Learn authentic Chinese from those who live and breathe the culture.

You will not only master the language but also gain a deeper understanding of China’s culture, customs, and history.
FAQs
1. How do Chinese people actually greet each other?
A common way for Chinese people to greet someone is by saying “你好” (nǐ hǎo), which means “hello.” In more formal situations, especially when addressing elders or superiors, Chinese people use “您好” to show respect.
In casual settings, friends might greet each other with “嘿” (hāi), similar to “hi” in English. Additionally, it’s common to ask about someone’s well-being with questions like “你好吗?” (Nǐ hǎo ma?), meaning “How are you?” Greetings may also include inquiries about family or work, reflecting genuine interest in the other person.
2. How do you answer to a Chinese greeting?
Responding to a Chinese greeting typically involves returning the same phrase. For instance, if someone says “你好” (nǐ hǎo), you can reply with “你好” as well, but it’s no standard answer. If someone greets you with “您好” (nín hǎo), responding with “您好” shows respect. However, there is a subtle difference in their usage and level of formality. “您好” is often used for the first meeting.
It’s also common to follow up with a question about the other person’s well-being, such as “你好吗?” (Nǐ hǎo ma?), which means “How are you?” This not only acknowledges the greeting but also demonstrates politeness and engagement in the conversation.
3. How do you say bye in China?
In Mandarin Chinese, a common way to say goodbye is “再见” (zàijiàn), which translates to “see you again.” This phrase is widely used in both formal and informal settings. For more casual farewells, people might simply say “拜拜” (bàibài), a borrowed term from English that conveys a friendly tone.
In certain contexts, people may also use phrases like “保重” (bǎozhòng), meaning “take care,” especially when parting from someone for an extended period. Overall, the choice of farewell can vary based on the relationship and situation, but “再见” remains the most standard expression. If you are interested in how to say and write “No” in Chinese, please check that article.

Codes & Standards
DNV updates physical environment standards
New NIAHO standards going into effect this month impact life safety, emergency management and more
Apr 14, 2025
|
Leah Hummel
DNV Healthcare USA Inc., has announced updates to its National Integrated Accreditation for Healthcare Organizations (NIAHO®) accreditation requirements with revision 25-0, set to take effect on April 28. The changes include significant updates to the Physical Environment (PE) section. Below is an overview of the revisions.DNV updatesPE.1 Facility. Previous editions of the NIAHO required compliance with Chapter 12 of the National Fire Protection Association’s NFPA 99, Health Care Facilities Code, 2012 edition, related specifically to emergency management. Revision 25-0 exempts Chapter 12 from the requirements, with additional clarifications found in PE.6. It also specifies that Chapter 13, Security Management, is not required by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) Conditions of Participation (CoP) but remains a requirement unique to DNV. This means CMS waivers for NFPA 99 cannot be applied to Chapter 13.Construction and repair activities have new guidance. Designs must adhere to the Facility Guidelines Institute’s Guidelines for Design and Construction documents. The new requirements also state that risk assessments and mitigation processes, including infection control risk assessments and alternative life safety measures (ALSM), are mandatory. These revisions also include references to NFPA 241, Standard for Safeguarding Construction, Alteration and Demolition Operations, for ALSM alongside the 2012 editions of NFPA 101®, Life Safety Code® and NFPA 99, and state and local building and fire codes. When conflicts arise, the most stringent requirements apply.PE.2 Life Safety Management System. Inspection frequency for portable fire extinguishers has been clarified. They must be checked once per calendar month, not exceeding 31-day intervals. Definitions for longer-term maintenance frequencies have also been standardized:Three years: Conducted once every 36 months, with a minimum of 30 months and a maximum of 40 months.Five years: Conducted once every 60 months, with a minimum of 54 months and a maximum of 66 months.PE.3 Safety Management System. A new requirement mandates organizations to establish a respiratory protection program (RPP) in compliance with Occupational Safety and Health Administration regulations.PE.5 Hazardous Material Management System. Organizations are now required to comply with NFPA 99-2012, 15.13.3, which pertains to fire prevention related to the use of germicides and antiseptics.PE.6 Emergency Management System. Compliance with NFPA 99, Chapter 12, Emergency Management, is no longer required under revision 25-0, although it remains an acceptable method of compliance as long as all other PE.6 requirements are met. Instead, new requirements align with CMS State Operations Manual, Appendix Z, regarding emergency preparedness. These changes are extensive and warrant careful review.PE.7 Medical Equipment Management System. The updated requirements clarify that medical equipment management system requirements apply to equipment owned by the organization in addition to rental or physician-owned equipment.More resourcesThe updates to DNV's NIAHO Accreditation Requirements in revision 25-0 introduce significant changes across multiple chapters, including increased clarity, new requirements and alignment with CMS CoP. Organizations accredited by DNV are encouraged to carefully review the changes to ensure adherence to the revised requirements.For more detailed information about these updates, visit the following resources:DNV’s NIAHO Accreditation Requirements for HospitalsDNV’s NIAHO Accreditation Requirements for Critical Access Hospitals

Codes & Standards
DNV changes its accreditation process
The accrediting organization updated its surveying requirements for documentation access and review
Dec 15, 2024
|
ASHE Staff
The standards application and interpretation team at DNV Healthcare USA Inc. has updated the 2024 NIAHO Program Accreditation Process with significant changes to the survey team arrival procedures. Effective immediately, the survey team leader will present a list of requested documents upon arrival to the facility. The health care organization must then provide the requested documents within three hours. This new requirement emphasizes the importance of hospitals having all necessary physical environment documentation readily accessible. Failure to produce these documents within the specified timeframe may result in a nonconformance, impacting the hospital's accreditation status. Hospitals should ensure that their document management systems are efficient, and that staff are prepared to quickly locate and provide the required documents to facilitate a smooth and compliant survey process. These changes, resulting from the critical access hospital program evaluation, apply to all hospital types. For more details, view the updated 2024 NIAHO Program Accreditation Process.

Checklist
DNV Healthcare updates accreditation standards
Plus: The Joint Commission clarifies spare breakers stance, shares top compliance challenges; EPA issues final rule on ethylene oxide emissions
May 31, 2024
|
HFM Staff
DNV Healthcare updates accreditation standardsDNV Healthcare USA Inc. made several revisions to its National Integrated Accreditation for Healthcare Organizations® (NIAHO®) standards. Revision 23-0 includes new requirements and a deleted requirement for NIAHO’s Physical Environment Chapter. Changes include updated interpretive guidance for annual evaluations of management plans and facility equipment maintenance. Revision 24-0 focuses on ligature risk, with a new standard added to the Patient Rights (PR) Chapter. The standard, PR. 10, Care in a Safe Setting: Patients at Risk of Harm to Self or Others, covers four new requirements.The Joint Commission clarifies spare breakers stanceThe National Fire Protection Association’s Healthcare Interpretation Task Force (HITF) discussed the issue of spare electrical breakers and whether they should be switched to the “on” or “off” position last summer. The HITF’s position is that because NFPA 70®, National Electrical Code®, does not dictate a particular position, spare breakers are permitted to be switched “on” or “off” without any code violation. However, The Joint Commission has clarified its stance on the issue, stating that the organization sees it as a safety issue rather than a code issue and therefore requires its accredited organizations to toggle their spare electrical breakers onto the “off” position. Facilities that are not accredited by The Joint Commission should reference the HITF decision.EPA issues final rule on ethylene oxide emissionsThe Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) issued a final rule that will require significantly reduced emissions from commercial facilities that sterilize medical devices and other equipment using ethylene oxide (EtO) gas. Among other changes, the rule will require continuous monitoring and reporting of EtO emissions and strengthen requirements for certain types of emissions that are not currently regulated, such as room air emissions. In a departure from the proposed rule, the EPA will give commercial sterilizers additional time to comply with the requirements and will apply emission percentage reduction targets that vary by a facility’s level of EtO emissions. The final rule will be published in the Federal Register.The Joint Commission shares top compliance challengesThe Joint Commission has shared the top five compliance challenges from eight of its accreditation programs based on surveys conducted in 2023. Although the top five requirements and corresponding elements of performance (EP) most frequently listed as noncompliant vary across the programs, there are commonalities. For instance, the Ambulatory Care, Critical Access Hospital and Hospital programs all had EPs related to control of airborne contaminants as one of their top five compliance challenges. Standards for performing intermediate and high-level disinfection and sterilization of medical equipment, devices and supplies were additional requirements frequently cited as noncompliant across several of the programs.

How to Say Hello in Chinese
By
Greg Rodgers
Greg Rodgers
Greg Rodgers is a freelance writer and photographer from Kentucky. He's been covering all things Asia for TripSavvy since 2010.
Travel's editorial guidelines
Updated on June 23, 2024
In This Article
View All
In This Article
The Difference Between Chinese and Mandarin
The Easiest Way to Say Hello
Saying Hello in Formal Occasions
Simple Responses in Chinese
How to Say Hello in Cantonese
Should I Bow When Saying Hello in Chinese?
How to Say Cheers in Chinese
Photo: Maremagnum / Getty Images
Knowing how to say hello in Mandarin and Cantonese allows you to properly greet more than 1.4 billion people who speak the languages. These basic greetings will help in your travels through China, Hong Kong, Macau, and Taiwan and will be understood in Mandarin- and Cantonese-speaking communities worldwide. Mandarin is the most widely spoken language in the world, so knowing how to say "hello" is extremely useful!
Mandarin and Cantonese are notoriously difficult languages to master, and native speakers of those languages know this and appreciate the effort. A relatively short word can take on completely different meanings depending on which of the four tones in Mandarin is used (or six tones in Cantonese!). Pinyin, a Romanization system for the Chinese characters, makes it easier to "read" Chinese, but you'll need to leave your English pronunciation habits behind.
Fortunately, tones aren't much of an issue for learning simple ways to say hello in Chinese. Context helps. You'll always be understood and will get plenty of smiles for the effort, particularly if you utilize a few tips for communicating with Chinese speakers.
TripSavvy / Ellie Yang
The Difference Between Chinese and Mandarin
Mandarin is a form of Chinese spoken in Mainland China. Although there are eight dialects and more than 200 languages spoken in China, Mandarin is the common language, spoken by nearly everyone. Cantonese is spoken in the provinces of Guangdong and Guangxi and in Hong Kong and Macau. You will encounter Mandarin while traveling in Beijing, and because it is the "speech of officials," knowing how to say hello in Mandarin is useful everywhere you go.
Mandarin has four tones:
First tone: flat (mā means "mother")Second tone: rising (má means "hemp")Third tone: falling then rising (mǎ means "horse")Fourth tone: falling (mà means "scold")No tone: Ma with a neutral/no tone turns a statement into a question.
Tones can entirely change the meaning of a word. As the above example with (ma) shows, using the wrong tones at the wrong times can cause serious confusion.
As for reading and writing, don't feel bad if you're baffled when confronted with Chinese characters—they're difficult! That's why we begin by learning how to use Pinyin.
The Easiest Way to Say Hello in Chinese
Ni hao (pronounced "nee haow") is the basic, default greeting in Mandarin Chinese. It is written as 你好 (nǐ hǎo). Ni hao's meaning is, literally, "you OK/good."
Although both words in Pinyin are marked as third tone (nǐ hǎo), the pronunciation changes a bit because two third tones occur back to back. In this instance, the first word (nǐ) is pronounced with a second tone that rises in pitch while the second word (hǎo) keeps the third tone and is pronounced with a "dip," a falling-then-rising tone.
Some people, particularly in Taiwan, choose to enhance the greeting by adding the interrogative "ma" to the end to form "ni hao ma?" Turning "you good" into a friendly "how are you?" But this isn't used as often in Beijing as language guides seem to think it is. When traveling in mainland China, a simple ni hao will suffice!
You will probably hear "hi" and "hello" often when being greeted as a Westerner in Beijing. You can reply with ni hao for practice and to be polite.
Saying Hello in Formal Occasions
Following the concept of saving face in Asia, elders and those of higher social status should always be shown a little extra respect. Adding just one additional letter (ni becomes nin) will make your greeting a bit more formal. Use nín hăo (pronounced "neen haow")—a more polite variation of the standard greeting—when greeting older people. The first word used is still a rising tone.
You can also make nin hao into "how are you?" by adding the question word ma to the end for nin hao ma?
Simple Responses in Chinese
The easiest reply to ni hao is offering a ni hao in return, but taking the greeting one step further is sure to get a smile during the interaction. Regardless, you should reply with something—not acknowledging someone's friendly ni hao is bad etiquette.
Hǎo: goodHěn hǎo: very goodBù hǎo: not good (bad)Xiè xie: thank you (pronounced similar to "zh-yeh zh-yeh" with two falling tones) is optional and can be added to the end.Nǐ ne: and you? (pronounced "nee nuh")
A simple greeting sequence could proceed like this:
You: Ni hao! (hello)
Friend: Ni hao ma? (how are you?)
You: Wo hen hao! Xie xie. Ni ne? (I am very good, thanks. And you?)
Friend: Hao. Xie xie. (Good. Thanks.)
How to Say Hello in Cantonese
Although it seems similar to Mandarin, Cantonese is actually quite different with six different tones instead of four. However, when it comes to greetings, saying hello in Cantonese is quite similar to Mandarin. Neih hou (pronounced "nay hoe") replaces ni hao. Both words have a rising tone.
Note: Although neih hou ma? is grammatically correct, it is uncommon to say this in Cantonese.
A common response in Cantonese is gei hou which means "fine."
Given Hong Kong's English history, you'll often hear "ha-lo" as a friendly hello! But reserve "ha-lo" for casual and informal situations. All other times, you should be saying neih hou.
Should I Bow When Saying Hello in Chinese?
No, absolutely not. Unlike in Japan where bowing is common, people tend to only bow in China during martial arts, as an apology, or to show deep respect at funerals. Many Chinese people opt to shake hands, but don't expect the usual firm, Western-style handshake. Eye contact and a smile are important.
Although bowing in China is rare, make sure you return one if you receive a bow. As with bowing in Japan, maintaining eye contact as you bow is seen as a martial arts challenge!
How to Say Cheers in Chinese
After saying hello in Chinese, you may end up making new friends—particularly if you're at a banquet or in a bar. Be prepared; there are some rules for proper drinking etiquette. You should certainly know how to say cheers in Chinese! The default toast in China is ganbei (sounds like: “gon bay”) which literally means "dry cup."
Along with knowing how to say hello, learning some useful phrases in Mandarin before traveling in China is a good idea.

标题: 廖望坤：中国高等教育质量评估体系简论 - 搜狐
URL: https://www.sohu.com/a/837561115_121021767
内容:
廖望坤：中国高等教育质量评估体系简论_认证_政府_进行

廖望坤：中国高等教育质量评估体系简论
2024-12-19 19:36
来源:
现当代管理科学
发布于：广西壮族自治区
廖望坤简介：廖望坤先生系中国数学奥林匹克教练、数学教学名师、数学史专家。中国高等教育质量评估体系是保障高等教育质量、推动高等教育高质量发展的重要机制，《中国高等教育质量评估体系简论》系廖望坤先生近年来对中国高等教育质量评估体系的最新研究成果，展现了宏观研究视角和独特科学见解。以下是廖望坤先生著作《中国高等教育质量评估体系简论》的部分章节和重要结论。
一、中国高等教育质量评估体系的发展背景
党的二十大报告要求进一步加快建设高质量教育体系，“高质量发展” 成为新时代我国高等教育发展的新目标和主旋律。从我国高等教育评估制度的演进来看，自 20 世纪 80 年代以来，其对保障高等教育质量发挥了积极作用，但发轫于计划经济时代、政府主导的评估制度也出现不少问题，引发了学界对评价者行为的反思。元评估等问题日益进入高等教育质量评估研究者的视野。
二、中国高等教育质量评估体系的构成
（一）外部质量评估
政府评价：政府在高等教育质量评估中发挥着重要作用。中国的第一次本科教学评估本质上是政府主导的对高等教育机构的问责制度，即行政问责。中国高等教育质量评估体系中，政府评价发挥着重要作用。政府通过多种方式对高等教育质量进行评估，以下是具体的方式阐述：
1、政策引导与制度设计
政府通过制定相关政策和制度来规范高等教育质量评估。例如，《教育部关于普通高等学校本科教学评估工作意见》提出了 “五位一体” 高等教育评估制度，对我国高等教育评估制度进行整体设计。教育部下发《关于深入推进管办评分离促政府职能转变的若干意见》，该文件引起高等教育界对政府职能转变、高校自主办学权实现途径、第三方教育评估机构建设以及家长、用人单位及利益相关者参与评估等方面的进一步关注，为我国 “五位一体” 高等教育评估制度的具体实施起到政策指引作用。
展开全文
2、行政性评估
在评估工作开展初期，政府集权型的自上而下的行政性评估模式极大地促进了中国高等教育评估事业的发展。这种模式下，政府通过制定评估标准、组织评估专家团队、对高校进行实地考察等方式，对高等教育质量进行监督和评估。
3、转变管理理念与领导方式
政府方面转变管理理念，转变领导方式。从传统的直接管理向宏观管理转变，注重引导高校建立自我评估意识和完善自我评估制度。通过政策引导和资源配置，鼓励高校积极提升教育质量，而不是单纯依靠行政命令进行管理。
总的来说，中国高等教育质量评估体系中政府评价的方式是多样化的，通过政策引导、行政性评估以及转变管理理念等方式，政府在保障高等教育质量方面发挥着重要作用。同时，政府也在不断探索新的评估方式，以适应高等教育发展的新要求。
质量认证：外部质量保证体系可以采取认证或鉴定的形式，具体取决于政府干预的程度。
质量认证网络分布
质量认证是一种由第三方机构进行的活动，旨在证实某一产品或服务符合特定的标准或规范性文件。这种认证可以分为产品质量认证和质量体系认证两种类型。产品质量认证的对象是特定产品（包括服务），而质量体系认证的对象则是企业的质量体系或质量保证能力。认证的依据通常是产品（服务）质量符合指定的标准要求，或是企业的质量体系符合指定的质量保证标准要求。认证的结果通常以颁发产品认证证书和认证标志，或是质量体系认证证书来证明。
外部质量保证体系的认证形式
外部质量保证体系的认证形式主要取决于政府干预的程度。在一些情况下，如安全认证，政府会实行强制性监督管理，这意味着某些产品必须通过法律、行政法规或规章规定的强制执行认证，否则不允许生产、销售或进口和使用。这种形式的认证是为了确保产品涉及的广大人民群众和用户的生命和财产安全。
认证与鉴定的区别
虽然认证和鉴定都涉及到对产品或服务质量的评估，但它们之间存在一些关键的区别。认证通常是一个更为正式和系统的过程，涉及到一系列的申请、评审、检查和批准步骤。而鉴定可能更加灵活和多样化，可能包括实验室测试、现场检查等多种形式。此外，认证的结果通常会有一个公开的认证名录，而鉴定的结果可能更多地用于内部决策或作为采购决策的一部分。
外部质量保证体系的认证形式确实可以采取认证或鉴定的形式，具体取决于政府干预的程度。在强制性监督管理的情况下，如安全认证，企业必须通过认证来证明其产品或服务符合必要的安全标准。而在其他情况下，企业可能会选择进行鉴定来满足特定的市场需求或提升自身的质量信誉。无论是认证还是鉴定，它们都是确保产品和服务质量的重要手段。
（二）大学排名：大学排名也是外部质量评估的一种方式。
1、大学排名作为外部质量评估方式的意义
（1）提供比较依据
大学排名通过一系列指标对大学进行量化评估，使得不同大学之间可以进行横向比较。例如QS世界大学排名、泰晤士高等教育世界大学排名等，这些排名能够让学生、家长、教育者等直观地了解各大学在全球范围内的相对位置，为选择学校提供参考依据。像学生在考虑出国留学时，可以根据这些排名筛选出在学术声誉、教学质量等方面表现较好的学校。
（2）反映综合实力
教学方面
排名指标中的师生比例、学生满意度等可以反映大学的教学资源投入和教学效果。例如泰晤士高等教育世界大学排名中的教学质量评估，涵盖教学资源、师生比例和学生满意度等内容，能够体现大学在教学环节的整体水平。
科研方面
论文数量、论文引用频次、高被引论文数量等科研相关指标，可以反映大学的科研产出和影响力。像软科排名中的科学研究成果评估，包括论文数量、被引频次等，能体现大学在科研领域的实力，进而反映大学的整体学术水平和创新能力。
国际化程度方面
国际学生比例、国际教师比例、国际合作项目等指标能反映大学的国际化水平。例如QS世界大学排名中的国际学生比例，以及U.S.News世界大学排名中的国际化程度评估（包括国际学生和教师比例、国际合作项目等），国际化程度高的大学往往在全球范围内有更广泛的学术交流与合作，这也是大学综合实力的一种体现。
比利时鲁汶大学
（三）大学排名存在的局限性
1、指标的局限性
指标无法全面涵盖
不同的排名体系虽然包含了多种指标，但仍然难以完全涵盖大学的所有方面。例如一些大学在当地社区服务、文化传承等方面有突出贡献，但这些可能未被纳入排名指标体系中。
2、指标权重的争议
各个排名体系对不同指标赋予的权重不同，这可能导致同一所大学在不同排名中的位置差异较大。比如有的排名可能更侧重科研成果，而有的可能对教学质量赋予较高权重。
3、数据的准确性和真实性
数据收集的难度
大学的规模、学科多样性等因素使得准确收集数据面临挑战。例如一些大学有多个校区、众多学科领域，要全面收集每个学科、每个校区的数据并非易事。
数据造假的可能性
尽管排名机构会采取措施确保数据真实性，但仍不能完全排除个别大学为了提高排名而提供虚假数据的情况。
（四）不同类型的大学排名及其特点
1、QS世界大学排名
评估指标
包括学术声誉（通过学术界调查问卷评估大学在特定学科领域的学术声誉和知名度）、雇主声誉（通过雇主调查问卷评估大学毕业生就业竞争力和认可度）、师生比例、国际学生比例、学术论文引用等指标，还提供多学科专项排名。
影响力
在全球范围内知名度较高，被广泛用于学生、学者和大学机构之间的比较和选择，尤其对于关注学校国际声誉、就业竞争力等方面的学生有较大参考价值。
2、泰晤士高等教育世界大学排名
评估指标
评估教学质量（包括教学资源、师生比例、学生满意度等）、科学研究（论文数量、引用次数、研究经费等）、学术声誉、国际化程度（国际学生和教师比例、国际合作项目等）、知识转移（专利申请数量、技术转让收入等），也有学科排名和特殊排名（如社会责任排名、教学创新排名）等。
影响力
是全球最具影响力和权威性的大学排名之一，被广泛认可和引用，能较全面地反映大学在教学、科研、社会服务等多方面的表现。
3、U.S.News世界大学排名
评估指标
主要依据大学的学术声誉（通过学术界和雇主调查问卷评估全球范围内的声誉）、教学质量（师生比例、学生入学质量、毕业率等）、科研成果（论文数量、被引频次、高被引论文数量等）、国际化程度（国际学生和教师比例、国际合作项目等），并提供学科排名和特定学科领域（如商学院、工程学院）排名等。
影响力
在美国本土以及国际上都有一定影响力，对于想要了解美国大学在全球的地位以及大学在学术、教学、国际化等方面综合情况的人有参考意义。
4、软科（上海交通大学世界大学学术排名）
评估指标
主要以大学的科研成果和学术水平为评估指标，特别注重自然科学和工程技术领域，包括科学研究成果（论文数量、被引频次、高被引论文数量等）、学术声誉、高被引学者数量、高引用论文数量等。
影响力
在中国被广泛认可和引用，对于关注大学科研实力，尤其是自然科学和工程技术领域科研实力的人是重要的参考排名。
（五）内部质量评估
评估主体：包括高校自身、教师、学生等。
评估人员：由专业的评估人员和相关利益者组成。
评估标准：涵盖教学质量、科研水平、师资队伍等多个方面。
评估模式：采用多种评估模中国高等教育质量评估体系中的内部质量评估机制至关重要，它对于提升高等教育质量、促进高校内涵发展起着关键作用。以下是中国高等教育质量评估体系内部质量评估的有效机制应包括的方面：
1、自我调节评估机制
建立科学的自我评估指标体系：高校应根据自身的办学定位、人才培养目标和学科专业特点，制定全面、系统、可操作的自我评估指标体系。例如，包括教学质量、科研水平、师资队伍建设、学生发展等多个方面。通过对这些指标的定期监测和评估，高校能够及时发现自身存在的问题和不足，并采取相应的改进措施。如英国的大学在确保学术自治的传统下，建立了自我调节评估机制，重视学生的评估参与度。
开展常态化的自我评估活动：高校应将自我评估作为一项常态化的工作，定期对教学、科研、管理等各个方面进行全面评估。可以通过内部审核、教学督导、学生评教、同行评议等多种方式进行。例如，教学督导可以定期深入课堂听课，对教师的教学态度、教学方法、教学效果等进行评价，并及时反馈给教师，帮助教师提高教学质量。学生评教可以通过问卷调查、网上评价等方式，让学生对教师的教学、课程设置、教学管理等方面进行评价，为学校改进教学提供参考依据。
2、校外监察员制度
英国剑桥大学
引入校外专家参与评估：高校可以邀请校外专家作为监察员，对学校的教学、科研、管理等方面进行独立评估。校外专家具有丰富的经验和专业知识，能够从不同的角度为学校提供客观、公正的评价和建议。例如，在英国的高等教育质量保障体系中，校外监察员制度发挥了重要作用。校外监察员可以对学校的教学计划、课程设置、教学方法、考试评价等方面进行审查，确保学校的教学质量符合标准。
建立校外监察员的选拔和管理机制：高校应制定严格的校外监察员选拔标准和程序，选拔具有丰富教学经验、较高学术水平和良好职业道德的专家作为监察员。同时，应建立校外监察员的管理机制，明确监察员的职责、权利和义务，加强对监察员的培训和考核，确保监察员能够认真履行职责，为学校的内部质量评估提供有力支持。
3、学生参与评估机制
鼓励学生积极参与教学评估：学生是教学活动的直接参与者，他们对教学质量有着最直观的感受。高校应鼓励学生积极参与教学评估，通过问卷调查、网上评价、座谈会等方式，让学生对教师的教学态度、教学方法、教学效果等进行评价。例如，一些高校在每学期末都会组织学生进行网上评教，学生可以对教师的教学进行打分和评价，并提出自己的意见和建议。学校可以根据学生的评价结果，对教师进行考核和奖励，促进教师提高教学质量。
建立学生反馈机制：高校应建立学生反馈机制，及时了解学生的需求和意见。可以通过设立学生意见箱、开通学生热线、建立学生代表大会等方式，让学生能够及时反映自己在学习、生活中遇到的问题和困难。学校应认真对待学生的反馈意见，及时采取措施加以解决，为学生创造良好的学习和生活环境。
4、内部质量监控体系
构建完善的教学质量监控体系：教学质量是高校内部质量评估的核心内容。高校应构建完善的教学质量监控体系，包括教学计划制定、教学过程管理、教学效果评价等多个环节。例如，在教学计划制定环节，高校应根据社会需求和学科发展趋势，制定科学合理的教学计划，确保教学内容的先进性和实用性。在教学过程管理环节，高校应加强对教师教学行为的规范和管理，确保教师按照教学计划和教学大纲进行教学。在教学效果评价环节，高校应建立科学的评价指标体系，对学生的学习效果进行全面评价，及时发现教学中存在的问题，并采取相应的改进措施。
加强科研质量监控：科研水平是高校综合实力的重要体现。高校应加强对科研质量的监控，建立科学的科研评价体系，对教师的科研成果进行客观、公正的评价。例如，可以通过设立科研项目评审委员会、学术委员会等机构，对教师的科研项目申报、科研成果鉴定等进行严格审查，确保科研成果的质量和水平。同时，高校应加强对科研经费的管理，确保科研经费的合理使用，提高科研经费的使用效益。
5、持续改进机制
建立问题反馈与整改机制：高校在内部质量评估过程中，应及时发现问题并进行反馈。对于发现的问题，应制定详细的整改措施，并明确整改责任人、整改时间和整改目标。例如，对于教学质量不高的问题，学校可以组织教师进行教学培训、教学观摩等活动，提高教师的教学水平；对于科研成果质量不高的问题，学校可以加强对教师的科研指导，提供必要的科研条件和支持，鼓励教师开展高水平的科研活动。
定期进行内部质量评估总结：高校应定期对内部质量评估工作进行总结，分析评估结果，总结经验教训，为下一轮的内部质量评估提供参考依据。同时，高校应将内部质量评估结果与学校的绩效考核、职称评定、岗位聘任等挂钩，形成有效的激励机制，促进学校内部质量的持续提升。
综上所述，中国高等教育质量评估体系内部质量评估的有效机制应包括自我调节评估机制、校外监察员制度、学生参与评估机制、内部质量监控体系和持续改进机制等方面。这些机制相互配合、相互促进，共同为提高中国高等教育质量提供有力保障。式，如自我评估、同行评估等。
三、国家质量评估方案
本科教育质量评估（QAUE）和学科评估
本科教育质量评估（QAUE）和学科评估是中国两个最重要的国家质量评估方案。这两个方案的具体评估方法和预期影响分别如下：
评估方法：QAUE 和学科评估采用不同的评估方法，包括对教学设施、师资队伍、教学管理等方面的评估。
预期影响：旨在提高高等教育质量，促进高校的发展和改革。但实际效果可能不如预期高，被评估机构虽然积极响应外部要求，但实际对大学运营的影响有限。
四、中国高等教育质量评估体系的特点
（一）历史发展视角
我国高校扩招从上世纪末开始，随着高等教育大众化的普及，我国高校教育质量显现出下降趋势，教育资源也相对紧张，因此高等教育质量问题日益受到高校内部及政府和社会的关注。
基于历史发展顺序，动态梳理我国高等教育质量评估体系的形成历程。从内部评估来看，包括评估主体、人员、标准、模式等方面；从外部评估来看，涉及政府、高校、社会等主要方面，研究这三股重要力量对高等教育质量评估权利的分配与制衡。
（二）与其他国家比较
通过比较研究的方法，静态分析我国与法国、英国、美国的高等教育内部评估与外部评估情况。在分析高等教育质量评估内部体系中，从评估主体、人员、标准、模式等方面对高等教育质量评估的内部情况进行分析；在分析高等教育质量评估外部体系中，从政府、高校、社会几个主要方面分析高等教育质量评估的外部评估体系情况。
总结和提炼我国高等教育质量评估体系在发展过程中的特点，并抽象出我国内部和外部的高等教育质量评估体系在发展中的缺点。例如，与英国、法国、美国相比，我国高等教育质量评估体系在政府主导程度、社会参与度等方面存在不同。
我国高等教育质量评估体系的完善可以从英国、法国和美国的高等教育质量评估体系中汲取丰富的经验。以下是具体的分析：
1、英国高等教育质量评估体系及可借鉴之处
评估机构及职能：英国高等教育质量保障局（QAA）在保障高等教育质量方面起到关键作用。其主要职能包括对高等教育机构进行评估，确保教学质量和学术标准符合要求。英国高等教育评估呈现多元评估的特点，有内部评估与外部评估相结合、自我评估与他人评估相结合、综合评估与单项评估相结合、结果评估与后继跟踪评估相结合等多种形式。
评估内容和程序：评估内容涵盖教学过程、学习环境和学习结果等多个维度。英国最新实施的 “教学卓越框架” 首次将学生知情选择与就业质量相联系，首次以教学卓越作为质量评估的着力点，首次将教学质量的维度覆盖教学过程、学习环境和学习结果，首次将评估与奖励有机结合，首次将教学经费政策与评估结果直接挂钩。
对我国的启示：我国可以借鉴英国的多元评估模式，丰富评估形式，加强内部评估与外部评估的结合，注重教学过程、学习环境和学习结果的全面评估。同时，可以建立评估结果与奖励及经费政策的关联机制，以激励高校提高教育质量。
2、法国高等教育质量评估体系及可借鉴之处
评估制度特征：法国高等教育评估制度以 1984 年《萨瓦里法》为分界，经历了早期阶段和新阶段，在发展完善中逐步形成鲜明的国家特征。其特征主要有评估制度的时代性，一直追随着法国高教机构的定位与管理体制的变化；评估机构的相对独立性，保证评估各方面得到一个第三方机构的客观评价；评估时间的周期性，每 8 年完成一次对所有高等学校的评估；评估内容的整体性，重视对高等教育系统整体协调性和整个高等教育政策的有效性进行评估；评估类型的多样性，表现为内部评估与外部评估相结合、自我评估与他人评估相结合、综合评估与单项评估相结合、结果评估与后继跟踪评估相结合。
对我国的启示：我国可以学习法国评估制度的时代性，使评估体系能够与时俱进，适应不断变化的高等教育环境。同时，保持评估机构的相对独立性，确保评估结果的客观公正。建立周期性的评估机制，全面评估高等教育系统的整体协调性和政策有效性，丰富评估类型，实现多种评估方式的有机结合。
3、美国高等教育质量评估体系及可借鉴之处
评估制度特点：美国是第一个开展教育评估的国家，在现代高等教育评估制度发展方面处于领先地位。美国高等教育评估制度以明确的主体化原则推动高等教育评估制度的实现，以系统性的评估程序促进高等教育评估制度的实现，以及以政府职能的发挥促进高等教育评估导向性的实现。美国模式以民间评估为主导，突出 “教育成果” 等输出性指标、重视 “同行评议” 等主观性指标、弱化 “校友捐赠率” 等易操控指标。
对我国的启示：我国可以借鉴美国明确的主体化原则，明确各评估主体的职责和权利，推动评估制度的有效实施。建立系统性的评估程序，确保评估过程的科学规范。发挥政府在评估中的引导作用，同时避免评估的随意性。加强民间评估组织的作用，重视输出性指标和主观性指标，提高评估的全面性和客观性。
综上所述，我国可以从英国、法国和美国的高等教育质量评估体系中借鉴多元评估模式、保持评估机构独立性、建立周期性评估机制、与时俱进、明确评估主体职责、建立系统性评估程序、发挥政府引导作用和加强民间评估组织作用等方面的经验，以不断改进我国的高等教育质量评估体系。
五、中国高等教育质量评估体系的问题与改进方向
（一）存在的问题
政府主导的评估制度存在问题：虽然政府主导的评估制度在一定程度上保障了高等教育质量，但也出现了不少问题，如评估标准单一、评估过程缺乏透明度等。
外部质量评估的实际效果有限：如 QAUE 虽然被评估机构积极响应，但实际对大学运营的影响不如预期高。
内部质量评估的动力不足：高校内部对质量评估的重视程度不够，缺乏有效的内部质量评估机制。
（二）改进方向
建立社会问责制度：为了提高中国高等教育质量评估体系的有效性，必须建立社会问责制度。社会问责制度可以促进高校更加关注社会需求，提高教育质量。
加强内部质量评估：高校应加强内部质量评估，建立有效的内部质量评估机制，提高内部质量评估的动力和效果。
提高外部质量评估的有效性：改进外部质量评估方法，提高评估标准的科学性和合理性，增强外部质量评估的实际效果。返回搜狐，查看更多

本科教育教学审核评估来了！这些你都知道吗？（一）
2024-10-29 09:13
来源:
搜狐城市-河南
发布于：河南省
根据教育部《普通高等学校本科教育教学审核评估实施方案（2021-2025年）》等文件精神与要求，学校将于2025年5月-6月接受本科教育教学审核评估。
为便于全校师生深入了解和熟悉审核评估指标的内涵，准确理解与掌握审核评估的规范要求，现推出“本科教育教学审核评估应知应会知识问答”系列专题，欢迎大家持续关注。
01.什么是审核评估？
审核评估是我国一项持续推进了40年的高等教育质量保障制度，原则上，普通本科高校参加本科教学工作合格评估并获得“通过”结论5年后方可参加审核评估，所以审核评估是一个常态性的工作。一般5年一个周期。本轮审核评估时间为2021-2025年。教育部联合各省教育厅组织15-21位专家（外省专家不少于2/3，专家组组长由外省专家担任），依据教育部审核评估指标体系对高校人才培养各方面进行评估。评估方式采取2-4周的线上评估和2-4天的带问题进校评估相结合、定性与定量相结合、明察与暗访相结合的方式进行。
02.为什么要开展审核评估？
开展审核评估的目的在于通过组织专家定期督查、诊断、指导，引导督促高校遵循高等教育规律和本科人才培养规律，督促高校工作聚焦本科教育教学质量全面提升。
03.本轮审核评估的目标定位是什么？
一是推动高校明确办学定位，主动服务经济社会发展，突出特色，扎根地方，形成与区域和地方共赢发展的良好局面。二是推动高校进一步提升特色办学模式，打造特色优势专业，升级改造传统专业。三是推动高校进一步创新人才培养机制。强化创新能力培养，大力提升学生的创新创业能力，激发创新创业活力。四是推动高校进一步增强服务地方经济发展能力。打造成校地共生发展模式。
04.本轮评建工作高校应坚持的方针是什么？
以评促建、以评促改、以评促管、以评促强
05.审核评估与合格评估有什么区别？
合格评估针对的是有3届以本校名义招生的普通本科毕业生，且未参加过教学工作评估的各类新建普通本科学校（以下简称新建本科学校），包括经国家正式批准独立设置的民办普通本科学校。已有5届本科毕业生的新建本科学校应参加合格评估。凡因未达到评估条件而推迟评估的学校，在学校推迟评估期间，教育部将采取暂停备案新设本科专业、减少招生人数等限制措施。
审核评估针对的是普通本科高校参加本科教学工作合格评估并获得“通过”结论5年后方可参加审核评估。
二者区别：合格评估重点关注的是“有没有，合不合格，是否通过”的问题，高校参加并通过即可。而审核评估关注的不是是否通过，而是“办的好不好，有哪些需要改进的地方”，每5年一个周期，是一个长期的持续性工作。
06.往届审核评估不存在过不过的问题，是不是就不用担心了？
首次参加审核评估的前提是通过了合格评估，即已基本满足本科教育的合格要求，所以关注的重点转为高校的内涵发展、特色发展、创新发展上。但 首次参与审核评估的高校仍然要检查基本办学条件，防止高校通过合格评估后办学条件出现倒退。如果首次参加审核评估发现高校办学基本倒退或者存在较大问题，高校必须在最多两年的时间内整改，否则将对高校进行通报，并视情况给予限制招生、减少招生、停止招生、限制新专业申报等措施。如果专家组给出的问题较多、较大，两年的整改期学校各单位、教师工作压力均会较大增加。
展开全文
07.审核评估有哪些特点？
一是强调评估结果使用和督导复查，强调评估长牙齿。本轮评估结果不是是否通过的评判，而是提出一系列需要改进的问题及学校可供向其他高校推广的办学特色。同时，教育部督导局每年会组织教育部督导专家对通过合格评估和审核评估的高校进行数据抽检（不发通知、不打招呼、不听汇报、不用陪同接待、直奔基层、直插现场）。对于高校存在的问题，必须在两年内进行全面整改，存在的问题越多越严重，高校管理层、师生两年整改的压力和工作量就越大。对于整改不力的高校，采取通报、限制招生、减少招生、限制新专业申报、限制一流专业等申报，限制相关荣誉申报等措施。
二是强调评估的常态性和引导性。相对于合格评估而言，审核评估是一个定期开展的常态性工作。旨在定期组织各方面的专家帮助高校查找问题，指导高校不断提升育人成效，促进高校特色发展。
三是重视数据分析与数据核查。本次审核评估专家重点审查、分析各高校每年提交的教学基本状态数据、评估期间高校提交的1+3+3报告、学校提交的其他相关数据资料达标情况、数据变化趋势，并通过将高校数据与全国同类高校常模值（平均值）、高校自选常模值（3组，每组5-6所与本校定位较一致的高校数据均值）进行横向对比，查找高校差距。同时通过严格的教育部督导局督导复查核实数据的真实性。其目的在于通过相关指标倒逼各高校关注人才培养质量，通过横向对比防止各高校闭门造车，强化各高校对建设成效的重视。通过纵向对比强化学校对自身质量发展情况的关注。最终实现提升高校质量提升的目标。通过督导复查防止高校数据造假。
08.什么是1+3+3报告？
1+3+3报告指的是审核评估线上评估阶段专家重点审查的7份报告。具体如下图：
自评报告是7份报告中唯一由学校撰写、提供的报告。
本科教学状态数据分析报告由每年11月份各高校向教育部提交的国测平台数据自动生成，教育部数据中心依据自有指标进行分析，分析数据直接以报告的方式提交给评估专家，高校不参与。重点关注高校各主要数据近3-5年的变化趋势。同时提供相应常模数据分析，便于直观的分析、查找学校工作中的亮点和存在的问题。
在校生学习体验调查报告、教师教学体验调查报告由高校组织师生参与教育部调查问卷后，由教育部数据中心自行分析得出。并与自评报告相印证。
本科生就业数据分析报告由教育部从各高校每年上报教育部学生发展中心的数据自动分析生成，高校不参与。
本科毕业生跟踪调查报告、用人单位跟踪调查报告分别从毕业生、用人单位角度量化反映学校本科毕业生就业情况，是人才培养目标达成度的外部评价。
上述7份报告，除自评报告由学校提供，其余6份报告均由教育部相关部门根据相关数据自行分析后提交评估专家。其中专家关注的专业就业质量之一是学生在本专业相关领域就业情况，以及本专业学生在本专业领域的优秀代表，需要各专业注意。
专家通过7份报告的多元评价、相互验证，发现各报告之间的一致性和矛盾点，进而掌握高校数据的真实性和问题所在。然后带着问题有针对性的进行相关检查和进校督查。
09.新一轮审核评估专家组如何使用“1+3+3”报告的？
《自评报告》是专家组开展评估考察工作的主要依据之一。专家组聚焦落实立德树人根本任务，围绕参评高校自主选择的评估类型、评估指标和审核重点，主要审核参评高校“该说的是否说了”“说了的是否做了”“做了的是否有效”“无效的是否改了”等，初步判断各项指标的达成情况，形成对学校教育教学情况的总体印象。
专家组审读《本科教学状态数据分析报告》《在校生学习体验调查报告》《教师教学体验调查报告》三个教学报告，注重发展变化。关注参评高校教育教学状态的变化及发展趋势情况，包括专业调整情况以及专业招生规模的变化情况，专任教师数量、教育教学经费、实验室设施条件和校内外实训基地、学生发展等状态数据的变化趋势等。通过数据对比，分析学校质量保障措施是否发挥作用。
专家组审读《本科生就业数据分析报告》《本科毕业生跟踪调查报告》《用人单位跟踪调查报告》三个就业报告，注重个性差异。把参评高校放在所处的社会环境中进行综合分析，把握参评高校所处的地域和经济发展环境的特殊性和特色点、学校所背靠的行业发展状况，分析参评高校培养目标和社会需求的契合度，人才培养对社会的贡献度。
10.什么是教育部督导复查？
教育部督导局的督导复查（俗称回头看）是教育部督导局以随机抽查的方式，对高校评估整改情况进行检查。该项工作与合格评估或审核评估分属两项相对独立的工作，由教育部不同部门组织不同专家进行。有时与教育教学评估同时进行，有时分不同时间进行。
督导复查按照“不发通知、不打招呼、不听汇报、不用陪同接待、直奔基层、直插现场”的方式进行。一般高校会在督导进校前1-2天获知复查通知。专家进校后不听汇报，直接调阅相关资料或深入一线听课或获取其他信息。如：督导专家不听汇报，采取直接到教务处登录教务系统调阅相关资料，或登录人事处人事系统调阅人事资料等。相关结果直接汇报给教育部督导局并全国通报。
对突破办学规范和办学条件底线、评估整改落实不力、关键办学指标评估后下滑的高校，采取约谈负责人、减少招生计划、限制新增本科专业备案和公开曝光等问责措施。
11.相对于上一轮审核评估，本轮审核评估有哪些新变化？
相对而言，本次审核评估将“教学质量审核评估”扩展为“教育教学审核评估”，已不仅仅关注教学，而是关注育人全过程。重点强调以下几个方面：
一中心：将“立德树人”作为检验学校一切工作的根本准则。
两突出：突出以本为本；突出四个回归，引导五育并举。
三强化：学生中心，产出导向，持续改进。
五个度：人才培养目标的达成度，社会需求的适应度，师资和条件的保障度，质量保障的有效度，学生和用人单位的满意度。
【说明】以本为本：把本科教育放在人才培养的核心地位、教育教学的基础地位、新时代教育发展的前沿地位。高校领导注意力要首先在本科聚焦，教师精力要首先在本科集中，学校资源要首先在本科配置，教学条件要首先在本科使用，教学方法和激励机制要首先在本科创新，核心竞争力和教学质量要首先在本科显现，发展战略和办学理念要首先在本科实践，核心价值体系要首先在本科确立。
四个回归：一是回归常识（学生是要刻苦学的）。要围绕学生刻苦读书来办教育，引导学生求真学问、练真本领。对大学生要合理“增负”，提升大学生的学业挑战度，激发学生的学习动力和专业志趣，改变轻轻松松就能毕业的情况，真正把内涵建设、质量提升体现在每一个学生的学习成果上。二是回归本分（教师是要认真教的）。要引导教师热爱教学、倾心教学、研究教学，潜心教书育人。坚持以师德师风作为教师素质评价的第一标准，在教师专业技术职务晋升中实行本科教学工作考评一票否决制。三是回归初心（高校应是为党育人，为国育才的）。要坚持正确政治方向，促进专业知识教育与思想政治教育相结合，用知识体系教、价值体系育、创新体系做，倾心培养建设者和接班人。四是回归梦想（教育是应有利于强国的）。要推动办学理念创新、组织创新、管理创新和制度创新，倾力实现教育报国、教育强国梦。
12.本轮审核评估采取什么样的方式进行？
利用大数据平台等现代化技术，采取“线上+线下”相结合的方式进行。
线上：2-4周 。15-21位不同领域分工的评估专家在线审核“1+3+3报告”，调阅人才培养方案、毕业论文（设计）、试卷等电子材料，开展教师、学生线上座谈，对校领导、职能部门负责人、院部负责人、教学管理人员、专任教师进行访谈，线上听课。专家组成员覆盖政产学研各领域、德智体美劳五育和被评学校主干学科专业，特别强调配置具有学校党政工作经历的专家，对学校育人的全员、全过程、全方位进行评估。
线下：2-4天（首评高校一般4天）。结合线上评估发现的问题，选派数位专家，带着问题进校有针对性的（不是全面的、宽泛的）现场检查、听课。
13.新一轮审核评估入校专家组成员是如何产生的？
入校专家组由专家组组长根据线上评估情况，从线上评估专家中确定5-9位专家组成。确定原则为：专家组组长、副组长、青年教师和有较多存疑问题的专家；适当考虑入校评估重点任务和学校学科专业布局。项目管理员和至少1名秘书入校协助专家开展工作。一般情况下学生观察员也参与入校评估考察工作。
14.本轮审核评估对高校分类评估（二类四种：第一类1种，第二类3种）是什么意思？
为了鼓励各高校结合自身办学目标定位开展特色发展，避免千校一面，本次审核评估结合高等教育整体布局结构和高校办学定位、服务面向、发展实际将评估指标分为“二类四种”不同的类型，同时每类指标都设置若干可选项，高校可结合自身情况进行自主选择。
第一类审核评估针对具有世界一流办学目标、一流师资队伍和育人平台，培养一流拔尖创新人才，服务国家重大战略需求的普通本科高校。
第二类审核评估针对高校的办学定位和办学历史不同，具体分为三种：
二类一种：适用于已参加过上轮审核评估，重点以学术型人才培养为主要方向的普通本科高校；
二类二种：适用于已参加过上轮审核评估，重点以 应用型人才培养为主要方向的普通本科高校；
二类三种：适用于已通过合格评估5年以上，首次参加审核评估、本科办学历史较短的地方应用型普通本科高校。
我校2019年11月通过本科教学质量合格评估，本次是首次参加审核评估。按规定，我校本次应选择第二类第三种（俗称“Ⅱ类3种”，或“B3”）评估指标。
第二类审核评估重点考察高校本科人才培养目标定位、资源条件、培养过程、学生发展、教学成效等。
15.评估分类是给学校分等吗？
新一轮审核评估分类是针对高等教育普及化阶段的多样化需求提出的，为了满足不同类型高校对质量保障的需求。鼓励高校结合学校自身情况、目标定位、区域社会发展需求等情况自主选择适合的评估类型开展建设，更好的培养出适应社会发展需要的优秀人才，进而办出特色，避免盲目跟风、照抄照搬。并通过在各项指标中设置一系列的评估可选项供高校自由选择，鼓励一校一案，避免出现千校一面的现象，促进我国高等教育的健康发展。评估专家评估时，按照高校自主选择的评估类型对应的指标进行评估。
审核评估方案不是封闭的，而是开放的、动态的，审核评估以5年为期限，为高校的发展留下了开放性空间。高校可以在这个周期选择一种主流类型来接受评估，到了下一个周期，随着高校的发展，还可以根据学校的实际选择另一种类型。学校人才培养可以以一种类型为主，其他按另一种类型培养，即：以学术型人才培养为主要方向的高校，不排除有些专业是培养应用型人才；以应用型人才培养为主要方向也不排除有些专业是培养学术型人才。返回搜狐，查看更多
